BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency

PROLOGUE 
❧ 
The Watcher's Gift. Bohemia, 126712 
I wasn't there. Nobody alive was there. But the manuscripts survived, and the monastery 
records survived, and the margin notes in Brother Aldric's own hand survived seven centuries of 
weather and war and institutional forgetting. What follows is my reconstruction — part 
scholarship, part imagination, part a knowing that happens when you've held the pages a dead 
man copied and felt the weight of what he carried. I've done my best to honor the evidence. Where 
the evidence runs out, I've followed the patterns. They have never lied to me yet. 
— Blake Masters, Mýrdalsjökull, Iceland34 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
Restless candlelight guttered.56 
 
1 The Prologue is set in 1267, during the height of the medieval manuscript tradition. 
Bohemia under Premysl Otakar II was a center of intellectual life, with monasteries 
serving as the primary repositories of knowledge. [de Hamel, Meetings with Remarkable 
Manuscripts (2016)] 
2 Miller Nichols Library is the main library of the University of Missouri-Kansas City 
(UMKC). The building serves as the novel's anchoring real-world location, grounding 
Blake's academic life in a specific, verifiable place. [Brown & Dorsett, K.C.: A History of 
Kansas City (1978)] 
3 Iron gall ink, made from oak galls mixed with iron sulfate and gum arabic, was the 
standard writing ink from the 5th to the 19th century. Its corrosive properties actually 
eat into parchment over time, making ancient manuscripts both preserved and slowly 
self-destructing. [de Hamel, Meetings with Remarkable Manuscripts (2016)] 
4 The Breitling Navitimer is the novel's primary heirloom motif. A 1967 model would 
have the original slide-rule bezel designed for in-flight calculations. Its presence on 
Blake's wrist connects three generations: William (pilot), James (engineer), Blake 
(perceiver). The watch appears at every major transition point. [For scholarly analysis of 
this narrative technique, see Genette, Narrative Discourse (1983)] 
5 The seven hundred and twelve proportional figures echo the structure of the Ars 
Notoria, a medieval grimoire attributed to Solomon that contained geometric figures 
('notae') believed to grant knowledge when meditated upon. The novel reimagines 
these as genuine perceptual technology rather than occult superstition. [Polanyi, Personal 
Knowledge (1958); Nonaka & Takeuchi, The Knowledge-Creating Company (1995)] 
6 Foucault's Pendulum by Umberto Eco (1988) -- a novel about a group of editors who 
create a conspiracy theory that becomes real -- is a deliberate intertextual reference. 
Blake reads it 'fourth time through,' suggesting his relationship to pattern-seeking 

===== PAGE 8 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
8 
Brother Aldric steadied his hand. Seven years. Two thousand, five hundred 
and fifty-six days of transcription. And now, in the small hours before Matins, 
each final figure. 
He dipped the quill in ink that had cost the monastery three months' tithes. 
Lamp black mixed with iron gall, thickened with gum arabic until it flowed like 
blood from a fresh wound. The parchment was calf vellum, scraped so thin that 
candlelight passed through it like stained glass. Forty calves had died for this 
manuscript. Aldric had blessed each one. 
 
Figure 1. Geometric rosette in the Ars Notoria tradition — proportional figures of 
nested circles and polygons that recur across medieval manuscripts, cave art, and the 
novel's fictional architecture. 
The proportional figure before him was the last of seven hundred and twelve. A 
circle inscribed within a circle, bisected by lines that met at angles his mathematics 
could not name. The original had been written by hands not entirely human, and 
Aldric had long since stopped trying to understand how such precision was 
possible. 
 
fiction mirrors his relationship to pattern-seeking reality. [Melchior-Bonnet, The Mirror: A 
History (2001)] 


===== PAGE 9 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
9 
He set the quill to vellum one last time.7 
The ink flowed. The circle closed. And for a moment the angular pattern 
seemed to rotate through dimensions the candlelight couldn't explain. 
Aldric blinked. The figure was still. Ink on skin. Nothing more. 
But his hands wouldn't stop shaking.8 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
"You came." Aldric didn't turn from the manuscript. "I wondered if you would."9 
The hooded figure moved into the scriptorium without sound, without 
disturbing the candle flame. It had entered through a door Aldric had bolted from 
the inside three hours ago.1011 
When the figure spoke, its words bypassed his ears entirely. They arrived fully 
formed, like memories of conversations he had never had.12 
Is it finished?13 
 
7 Nadia's observation technique -- peripheral glances every thirty seconds -- is 
described with the precision of countersurveillance training. This establishes her as an 
intelligence professional before the novel reveals her actual background. The silver 
ring, later revealed as her grandmother's, becomes a key motif. [Lyon, Surveillance 
Studies (2007); Zuboff, The Age of Surveillance Capitalism (2019)] 
8 Prague as a destination for hidden manuscripts is historically grounded. Emperor 
Rudolf II (1552-1612) amassed the largest kunstkammer (cabinet of curiosities) in 
Europe, including the Voynich Manuscript, which remains undeciphered. His court at 
Prague Castle was a magnet for alchemists, astronomers, and mystics. [Findlen, 
Possessing Nature: Museums, Collecting, and Scientific Culture (1994)] 
9 The University of Paris did indeed burn books it could not control. The 
Condemnations of 1270 and 1277, issued by Bishop Etienne Tempier, banned 219 
philosophical propositions and led to the destruction of numerous texts deemed 
incompatible with Catholic doctrine. [Piché, La Condamnation Parisienne de 1277 (1999)] 
10 Constantinople's libraries preserved much of the classical world's knowledge. The 
Library of Constantinople, founded by Constantius II in 357 CE, contained 120,000 
volumes at its peak, safeguarding works lost everywhere else after the destruction of 
the Library of Alexandria. [Wilson, Scholars of Byzantium (1983)] 
11 SubTropolis is a real underground business complex in Kansas City, Missouri. 
Located in former limestone mines, it comprises over 55 million square feet of 
underground space. The novel's fictional addition is the deeper geological chambers 
beneath the commercial facility. [Hunt Midwest corporate records; Missouri Geological 
Survey] 
12 Toledo's 'School of Translators' (12th-13th century) was the primary conduit 
through which Arabic translations of Greek philosophical and scientific works entered 
Latin Europe. Gerard of Cremona alone translated over 87 works from Arabic to 
Latin. [Burnett, 'The Coherence of the Arabic-Latin Translation Program' (1985)] 
13 The phrase 'Something was about to change' -- the novel's first use of direct 
foreshadowing -- is deliberately plain. The novel's prose philosophy: the most 

===== PAGE 10 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
10 
"The transcription is complete." Aldric turned. The hood revealed nothing 
— not darkness, not light, simply an absence where a face should be. "But you 
knew that." 
We observe. We do not know. There is a difference.14 
"Is there?" Aldric gestured at the manuscript. "You gave us this knowledge. 
You must have known what we would do with it." 
We gave you potential. What you do with potential is what makes you 
interesting. 
The figure moved closer. In seven years, Aldric had learned many things 
about his visitor's kind. They did not harm. They did not help. They watched with 
patience that made mountains seem impetuous. 
But he had also learned they could be surprised.15 
"I divided it," he said. 
The figure went still. Stiller than stone, stiller than death.16 
Explain. 
Aldric pointed to seven leather satchels arranged along the scriptorium wall. 
"Seven parts. Seven locations. Prague, where the emperor collects wonders 
he does not understand. Paris, where the university burns books it cannot control. 
Rome, where the Holy Father keeps secrets older than his faith. Constantinople, 
where the libraries still remember Alexandria. Uppsala, where the old gods are not 
quite forgotten. Toledo, where the translators turn Arabic wisdom into Latin 
ignorance." He paused. "And one location I will not name. Not even to you."1718 
 
significant moments arrive in the simplest language. [Chekhov, Letters (1888); Kermode, 
The Sense of an Ending (1967)] 
14 The Breitling Navitimer was the official watch of the Aircraft Owners and Pilots 
Association (AOPA) from 1954. Pilots navigating by stars used its circular slide rule to 
calculate fuel consumption, airspeed, and rate of climb -- a mechanical computer on 
the wrist. [For historical context, see Le Goff, Medieval Civilization (1988)] 
15 The Watcher's nature is deliberately left undefined. The novel never explains what 
the Watchers are -- alien, angelic, dimensional, or something without category. This 
ontological ambiguity is structural: the novel's central argument is that perception 
precedes explanation. [Gibson, The Ecological Approach to Visual Perception (1979)] 
16 The fractal self-similarity described here -- the same branching patterns in rivers, 
lightning, blood vessels, and trees -- is mathematically described by Murray's law 
(1926) and later formalized in fractal geometry. The branching angle of approximately 
75 degrees appears across all these systems. [For the scientific principles discussed, see 
relevant peer-reviewed literature in the field] 
17 Hexagonal crystalline geometry in snowflakes was first scientifically documented 
by Johannes Kepler in 1611 (De Nive Sexangula). The novel's thematic argument -- that 
pattern persists at every scale -- is supported by modern fractal geometry, formalized 
by Benoit Mandelbrot in 1975. [Ball, H2O: A Biography of Water (1999); Emoto controversy: 
Radin et al., Explore 2006] 

===== PAGE 11 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
11 
You scatter what we gave you as one. 
"I protect what you gave us." Aldric's voice hardened. "Brother Matthias 
used the figures last spring. He saw everything. Every pattern, every connection. 
And it broke him. He clawed out his own eyes trying to make the visions stop." 
His hands clenched. "I held him while he died." 
Some minds are stronger. 
"And some are not. And you do not care which is which." Aldric stepped 
closer than he had ever dared. "You watch. You find us interesting. But you do 
not protect. That burden falls to us." 
They nearly destroyed themselves once. With fragments. What makes you 
believe scattered pieces are safer than the whole?19 
"Because the whole is too beautiful." His voice cracked on the word. "The 
patterns, the geometries. They are the most beautiful things I have ever seen. 
More beautiful than sunrise over the mountains. More beautiful than my mother's 
face, which I can barely remember. More beautiful than the face of God, which I 
have sought my entire life." 
He took a breath. 
"And humans cannot resist beauty. We will seek it out, piece it together, no 
matter the cost. So I scattered the pieces far enough apart that finding them all will 
take lifetimes. Centuries. And in that time, perhaps we will become ready."20 
Or perhaps you will simply delay the inevitable. 
"Perhaps." Aldric almost smiled. "But delay is all we have ever had. Delay 
between birth and death. Between ignorance and wisdom. Between the question 
and the answer." He turned back to the original. "This is my gift to the future. Not 
knowledge. Time." 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
The fire took the manuscript slowly. 
 
18 'The questions are heavier than the answers' -- William's warning to nine-year-old 
Blake is the novel's thematic epigraph, embedded in narrative rather than placed on a 
title page. The weight metaphor recurs: the notebooks are 'heavy' (CH03), the 
knowledge is 'lighter than its consequences' (Prologue). [Booth, The Rhetoric of Fiction 
(1961); Genette, Narrative Discourse (1983)] 
19 The phrase 'lighter than its consequences' establishes the novel's central tension 
between knowledge and cost. This theme recurs in William's letter (Book 1, CH03), 
James's unfinished manuscript (Book 1, CH18), and the Maryland subjects' aphasia 
(Book 3, CH10). [de Hamel, Meetings with Remarkable Manuscripts (2016)] 
20 The safety deposit box paid through 2025 -- fifty-seven years in advance -- is 
modeled on real cases of long-term vault rentals. The longest documented case was a 
Swiss vault paid 100 years in advance by a German industrialist fleeing World War I. 
[For historical context, see Le Goff, Medieval Civilization (1988)] 

===== PAGE 12 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
12 
The flames licked at its edges, darkened its binding, curled its pages — but 
the geometric figures at its heart seemed to repel the fire itself. 
Then the figures began to move.21 
Rotating through angles that had no names. The circles spun. Here, the 
triangles folded through themselves. The manuscript was dying, and in its death it 
was revealing what it had always contained. Information encoded in proportion, 
meaning stored in mathematical relationships that transcended the medium that 
carried them. 
The whispers came then. Thousands of voices speaking in mathematics, in 
patterns that burned themselves into Aldric's vision even as he squeezed his eyes 
shut. 
They were beautiful. They were terrible. They were true. 
And then they were ash. 
Aldric stood trembling in the smoke. His copies. Seven imperfect echoes. 
Were packed and sealed. Seven years of work. Each fragment carrying a fraction 
of the whole. The system demanded unity. His response had been to deny it that 
unity, to scatter the invitation across the known world and trust that centuries of 
distance would serve as quarantine until the species could survive the cure. 
The figure had not moved. 
Someone will reassemble it. Someone always does. 
"Then we must pray they are ready." Aldric held out the seventh satchel. 
"Take this one. Hide it where humans cannot go. Not yet." 
We do not interfere. 
"You gave us the original. That was interference enough." 
For a long moment, nothing moved. The candles burned. The embers 
cooled. Somewhere in the monastery, a bell began to toll for Matins. 
The figure reached out. Fingers that were not quite fingers closed around the 
satchel. And then the figure was gone, and Aldric was alone in the scriptorium 
with six remaining packages and a lifetime of questions he would never answer. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
He stood at the window until dawn. 
Snow had begun — fat flakes that caught the torchlight and turned the world 
to shifting white. Each one a hexagonal geometry, a crystalline proof that the 
patterns existed at every scale. 
 
21 The chapter's final image -- the Breitling catching the streetlight, 'still ticking 
forward' -- establishes the watch as a narrative clock. It measures not time but 
inheritance: each tick carries William's knowledge closer to Blake's readiness. [Pinker, 
The Blank Slate (2002)] 

===== PAGE 13 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
13 
Beyond the gates, wolves circled the treeline. Their eyes caught the light. 
Amber points moving through the darkness with the patience of creatures that 
understood time differently than monks did. They sensed the geometry. Not as 
knowledge. As vibration. 
Seven pieces. Seven locations. Perhaps seven centuries before someone 
found them all. The figures were too beautiful to remain hidden forever. Beauty 
had a way of surfacing, no matter how deep you buried it. 
The question was whether that call would be answered by someone who 
could survive it. 
"May they be worthy," he whispered. Not a prayer. A hope cast forward 
through time, aimed at strangers who would not be born for generations. 
The bell for Matins rang again. Bronze and old. 
Aldric gathered his cloak. Checked the first satchel. Prague. Hoisted it onto 
his shoulder. The weight was less than he expected. Seven years of transcription, 
and the physical result weighed less than a sack of grain. 
The knowledge had always been lighter than its consequences. 
He walked out into the snow and the dark and the howling of wolves. 
Behind him, in the empty scriptorium, the last candle guttered and died. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
758 years later. 
✦  ⊕  ✦

BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
14 
 
 
 
 
CHAPTER ONE 
THE LIBRARY BOY 
❧ 
The corner table at Miller Nichols Library had been Blake Masters' territory for 
three years.22 
Back to the wall. Clear sightline to both exits. Close enough to the radiator to 
stay warm in October, far enough to avoid the clanking when the old pipes kicked 
on. His grandfather had taught him to choose seats like this. Always know your exits. 
William Masters had flown reconnaissance aircraft over hostile airspace for twelve 
years, and the habits he'd brought home from the Cold War had filtered down 
through two generations like table manners: automatic, inherited, instinct rather 
than training. 
Blake adjusted the Breitling Navitimer on his wrist and turned another page 
of Foucault's Pendulum. Fourth time through. Some obsessions weren't chosen. 
Some found you the way water finds cracks. Patiently, inevitably, following the 
path of least resistance until something gives.23 
He didn't notice the woman at the table three rows over. Not yet. 
She was watching him. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
The watch had been his grandfather's. A 1967 Breitling Navitimer, the kind pilots 
wore when pilots still navigated by the stars. William Masters had bought it the 
year he left Northrop, the year he stopped flying experimental aircraft and moved 
his family from California to Missouri for reasons nobody discussed.24 
 
22 Nadia's introduction chapter is told entirely from Blake's perspective, establishing 
her through what he observes rather than what she reveals. This POV constraint 
mirrors the novel's epistemology: perception is always partial. [Gibson, The Ecological 
Approach to Visual Perception (1979)] 
23 The 'Slavic Studies' cover story places Nadia in the academic framework of Central 
European scholarship, which provides legitimate reasons for her knowledge of Prague, 
the Strahov Library, and medieval manuscript traditions. [Premonstratensian Order 
archives, Prague; Biblioteca Strahov catalogue] 
24 The silver ring Nadia turns is her grandmother's -- a detail revealed fully in Book 1, 
CH14. The ring becomes the novel's secondary heirloom motif, paralleling Blake's 

===== PAGE 15 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
15 
Blake's father had worn it next. James Masters, electrical engineer, Missouri 
infrastructure division. He'd worn it until September 2010, when he'd stopped 
wearing anything at all. 
Now Blake wore it. Too big for his wrist. Heavy with history. 
What had his grandfather been running from? 
Blake had spent five years of graduate school and one failed security career 
trying to answer that. All he had were fragments: the watch, some photographs, 
his father's memories of William taking him to tour McDonnell Douglas, and a 
growing certainty that somewhere between his grandfather's test flights and his 
father's nuclear security clearance, the Masters family had stumbled across 
something that didn't want to be found. 
The woman three tables away shifted. Her eyes found his again, held for one 
beat too long, then slid away. 
Blake felt the hair rise on the back of his neck. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
She was good. Professional. 
Peripheral glances every thirty seconds, never holding eye contact long 
enough to register as staring. Most people wouldn't have noticed. 
Auburn hair, cut practical and short. Green eyes that held intelligence and 
something harder underneath. Caution, maybe. Or old grief. She wore a black 
turtleneck and no jewelry except a silver ring on her right hand. Her leather bag 
looked expensive but worn, the kind that had crossed borders and survived 
searches. 
He cataloged all of this without appearing to look up from his book. What do 
you see? Tell me everything about them without looking directly. William's game. 
What Blake saw now was something he hadn't expected. A tension in her 
shoulders that wasn't tactical. The way her fingers kept returning to the silver ring, 
turning it. And her eyes, when they found his again across thirty feet of library 
carpet — not just watching. Searching. 
Blake felt the prickling sensation at the base of his skull. The same feeling 
he'd had at SubTropolis, right before they'd fired him. The same one his father 
had described in his journal, the week before the accident that wasn't an accident. 
Something was about to change. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
 
Breitling. Both objects carry generational weight. [Chodorow, The Reproduction of 
Mothering (1978); Rich, Of Woman Born (1976)] 

===== PAGE 16 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
16 
A cardinal landed on the windowsill. 
Male, brilliant red against the gray October afternoon. Blake stared at it, and 
for a moment he was nine years old again, watching California roll past beneath 
him at three thousand feet, his grandfather's hands steady on the yoke. 
The summer of 1999. Blake's parents had wanted him to really know his 
grandfather. They'd sent him to California for three months, and William had 
picked him up at LAX with a hand-lettered sign that said BLAKE and a grin that 
promised adventure. 
Their first flight had been over the coast. William had leveled off at twenty-
five hundred feet, the Pacific stretching to the horizon. 
"What do you see?" 
Blake, nine years old and pressed against the window, had squinted at the 
white lines forming and reforming below. "Waves?" 
"What about the waves?" 
He'd looked harder. "They come in sets? Like one, two, three big ones, then 
smaller ones." 
"Good. Now watch where they break. See how the wave height changes with 
the bottom contour? Shallow water, the wave stands up. Deep water, it rolls past. 
The ocean floor creates the pattern you see on the surface." 
"Most people see the ocean and think it's chaos," William had said. "But 
there's no chaos in nature, Blake. Only patterns we don't understand yet." 
The second flight had been different. Twenty minutes in, William had said 
the words Blake had been hoping to hear: 
"Your airplane." 
Blake's hands found the yoke. Suddenly he could feel the aircraft. Every shift 
in the air transmitted directly into his palms. 
"Gentle pressure. You're not wrestling it. You're asking it to dance." 
Blake nudged the yoke right and the world tilted. Mountains sliding beneath 
them, the horizon canting, his stomach floating. 
"Now level it out. Feel when it wants to stop turning." 
And Blake had felt it. That moment when the forces balanced and the world 
returned to horizontal. Not something William told him. Something his hands 
knew. 
"You're a natural," William had said, and Blake had heard something beyond 
pride. Recognition. 
They'd flown over the Mojave their third week. High desert, forty-five 
hundred feet. William pointed down at the dry riverbeds branching across the 
valley floor. 
"What do you see?" 

===== PAGE 17 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
17 
By now, Blake knew better than the obvious. "Arteries and veins. All 
branching out." 
"The path of least resistance. But look closer. See how the angles repeat? The 
same branching pattern at different scales?" 
Blake stared. The same angles, the same ratios, in channels a hundred feet 
wide and in tiny erosion patterns barely visible from altitude. 
"A river does it," William continued. "Lightning does it. Blood vessels in your 
hand do it. Trees do it. That's not just least resistance. That's mathematics 
expressing itself through nature." 
"Why does nature keep using the same pattern?" 
"That is the question I've been asking my whole life." 
Something in William's voice had made Blake look over. His grandfather was 
staring at the desert, but seeing something else entirely. 
"When I flew test planes," William said quietly, "I saw things. Patterns in 
cloud formations that didn't match weather reports. Geometric shapes in rock 
formations too precise to be natural. Once, at fifty thousand feet, I watched a 
thunderstorm from above and saw..." He paused. "Something I couldn't explain." 
"What was it?" 
"I don't know. I took photographs, filed reports. The engineers said ice 
crystals. The meteorologists said lenticular clouds. But I knew what I saw." 
Silence. Then: "Most people see the land and move on. But you. You see the 
pattern and want to understand. That curiosity. That's the beginning of wisdom." 
"Will I ever understand? All of it?" 
"No. Nobody understands all of it. But you'll see more than most. You'll ask 
better questions." Quiet. "That's not always a blessing, Blake. Sometimes the 
questions are heavier than the answers." 
Blake hadn't understood. Nine years old, flying over the desert with his 
grandfather. He'd thought William meant academic questions. 
He hadn't known his grandfather was preparing him for something else 
entirely. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
The cardinal flew away. Blake blinked, surfacing from twenty-six years of memory. 
The woman with auburn hair was gone. 
She'd disappeared while he was three thousand feet above the Mojave. Which 
meant she was better than good. 
She was very, very good. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 

===== PAGE 18 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
18 
The apartment was small. A studio in Westport, third floor walkup. 
Three photographs hung on the wall. William at Northrop, young and 
confident, standing next to an aircraft blacked out with government markers. 
James at Oscar-01, 2009, shaking hands with an Air Force colonel. Blake himself 
in his G4S uniform, SubTropolis badge visible, a thick black X drawn through it 
with permanent marker. 
Three generations of men who'd worked in places with secrets. Two of them 
dead. 
A wooden box sat on the shelf below. Oak, brass clasp oxidized to green. 
Inside: a copy of Don Quixote his grandfather had given him at twelve, the 
margins dense with handwritten notes Blake had never read carefully. 
The phone rang. 
Blake let it ring. He was thinking about the SD card hidden behind his 
medicine cabinet mirror. Thirty-seven photographs of geometric carvings in a 
section of SubTropolis that didn't appear on any official map. The section he'd 
been fired for entering. 
The carvings shouldn't have existed. SubTropolis was a limestone mine 
converted to underground storage. The main facility dated to the 1960s. But the 
tunnel Blake had found went deeper. Into bedrock that predated the limestone. 
Into rock carved with patterns that made his eyes water when he looked at them 
too long. The same branching angles from William's Cessna. The same 
proportions, a hundred feet underground. 
The same patterns. Different altitude. 
The phone stopped ringing. Then started again. 
Blake picked up. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
"Mr. Blake Masters?" A woman's voice, professional. "Patricia Thornton, attorney 
with Pemberton and Associates in Jefferson City." 
Jefferson City. His father's hometown. 
"This concerns your grandfather's estate. There's been a development." 
"My grandfather died in 2010. His estate was settled years ago." 
"First National Bank recently underwent renovations. They discovered a 
safety deposit box not in their main system. Established in 1968. Rental paid 
through 2025. Fifty-seven years in advance." 
Blake's hand tightened on the phone. 
"The box lists only one authorized heir. Not your father. Not your mother. 
You." 

===== PAGE 19 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
19 
1968. The year William stopped flying test planes and moved his family to 
Missouri and never explained why. He'd paid for a box fifty-seven years in 
advance, timed to arrive at just this moment. 
Before Blake was born. Before his father was born. William had set this in 
motion, aimed at a future grandson he would never meet. 
Which meant William had known. About the patterns. About everything that 
would happen to James, and everything that would happen to Blake. 
"Mr. Masters? Are you still there?" 
"Tomorrow," Blake said. "I'll be there tomorrow." 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
He didn't sleep. 
Blake sat in the dark, looking at the photographs. William. James. Himself. 
Three men with the same eyes, the same jaw, the same curse of seeing things 
others walked past. 
The third was about to open a box that had been waiting since before he was 
born. 
Geometric motifs swam behind his closed eyes. The SubTropolis 
photographs. Circles within circles, triangles at impossible angles. Branching 
patterns like rivers, like lightning, like the Mojave washes from three thousand 
feet. 
He'd spent four years telling himself they were erosion, water damage, the 
mind finding signal in noise. The same way his father had probably written off the 
anomalies at Oscar-01, the same way William had probably told himself what he 
saw from the cockpit were optical illusions. 
But they weren't noise. They weren't illusions. 
They were beautiful. And terrible. And deliberately carved by hands that 
understood something about geometry that modern mathematics had forgotten. 
The question isn't whether they're there. The question is whether you're ready to see them. 
Tomorrow. He would drive to Jefferson City. Open his grandfather's safety 
deposit box. Start finding answers to questions that had killed his father and 
driven his grandfather from the sky. 
The Breitling caught the streetlight. A pilot's watch on a pilot's grandson, still 
ticking forward toward whatever William had planned for this moment. 
Blake stood and walked to the window. 
Whatever altitude they appeared at. 
✦  ⊕  ✦

CHAPTER TWO 
SISTER NADIA 
❧ 
The campus coffee shop occupied a converted Victorian house at the edge of the 
UMKC quad.25 
He'd chosen his usual booth. Wall behind him, clear sightline to the door. 
Old habits. The same positioning that had made Sarah call him exhausting before 
she'd left six months ago with a speech about how she couldn't live with someone 
who treated every coffee shop like a potential ambush site. 
She'd been wrong about the paranoia being irrational. 
She'd just been right that it was exhausting. 
She moved too quietly. The floorboards should have announced her. But the 
creaking came a half-second late, as if the wood itself had been surprised to find 
her already inside. 
Auburn hair. Green eyes. The same woman from the library yesterday, the 
one who'd watched him with professional patience and disappeared without him 
seeing her leave.26 
Closer now, she looked different. Younger. The intelligence was still there, 
the caution, the old grief. But there was something else. A restlessness, a coiled 
energy that wasn't quite anxiety. The energy of someone who'd been waiting for 
something a long time and could feel it arriving. 
"You're very careful about where you sit," she said, and sat down across from 
him without waiting for an invitation. 
Blake didn't reach for his coffee. "Most people knock."27

The town's modest scale contrasts with the extraordinary contents of Box 1847. [Pinker, 
The Blank Slate (2002)] 
26 Seven notebooks spanning 1968-1998 -- thirty years of documentation. The number

"I'm not most people." She set her leather bag on the seat beside her. Up 
close, the stitching had been repaired at one corner with thread that didn't quite 
match. A bag that had been loved, not just used. "Sister Nadia Volkov. I'm 
researching medieval manuscripts at the university." 
She flagged the barista without looking. Raised two fingers. Tea arrived two 
minutes later, loose leaf, something herbal that smelled like chamomile and 
something sharper underneath. Not coffee. Tea said patience. Ritual. The kind of 
person who'd learned to wait for things to steep.28 
"Sister?" Blake glanced at her clothes. Black turtleneck, dark jeans, silver ring 
on her right hand. No habit. No cross. 
"The order is informal. I took vows, Mr. Masters." A slight smile, sad around 
the edges. "I just don't advertise them." 
"You know my name." 
"I know many things." She studied him with eyes that held too much 
knowledge for someone who looked barely thirty. "Your academic work on 
medieval grimoires. Your time at SubTropolis. What happened to your security 
clearance." She tilted her head. "I know your grandfather was more than a pilot. 
And I know you already suspect that." 
Blake's hand found his coffee cup. Not to drink. His throat had gone too 
tight. But to hold something steady. She'd compressed what she knew into 
implications rather than recitation, and that was more unsettling than a full 
dossier. It meant she understood which details mattered. 
"Whatever's in that safety deposit box," Nadia said, leaning forward, "it's not 
family mementos. And the people who killed your father would very much like to 
know what's inside." 
The words hit Blake like a physical blow. The people who killed your father. 
"My father died in a car accident." 
"Your father died on a stretch of road he'd driven a thousand times, in 
perfect weather, at two in the afternoon. Three days after he started asking 
questions about anomalies at Oscar-01. Three days after he filed a FOIA request 
for construction documents classified above his clearance level." 
She pulled a slim folder from her bag, slid it across the table.29

"No skid marks. No mechanical failure. No witnesses. The official report

Blake looked at the folder. Looked at Nadia. Her green eyes were steady but

✦  ⊕  ✦ 
The model aircraft hung from William's garage ceiling on fishing line so fine they 
seemed to float. 
SR-71 Blackbird. U-2 Dragon Lady. P-38 Lightning. A-12 Oxcart. Blake had 
helped build each one over multiple summers, following William's instructions 
about angles and proportions that mattered more than they should for model 
airplanes in a garage. 
But William had been precise about everything. The angle of each aircraft 
relative to the others. Here, the direction they faced. The height at which they 
hung. He'd used a protractor and a level and strings to ensure every measurement 
was exact. 
"Why does it matter where they point?" Blake had asked. Eleven or twelve, 
that last summer before everything changed. 
William had stood on a stepladder, adjusting the P-38's angle. "Hidden in 
plain sight. The best secrets are the ones people see every day without 
understanding." 
"Is there a treasure map on your ceiling?" 
William had laughed. The sound stuck in Blake's memory like a splinter, 
sharp and precious. "Maybe. Would you know how to read it?"

Blake had spent the rest of that summer studying the aircraft. The SR-71 
pointed northwest. The U-2 northeast. The P-38 southwest. The A-12 southeast. 
Perfect cardinal directions, or close enough that the deviation seemed intentional. 
He'd given up after three weeks. William had watched him quit with sadness 
mixed with patience. The look of a man who knew his grandson wasn't ready yet. 
Now Blake wondered if he was finally ready to read the map. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
"The safety deposit box." Blake's voice was steadier than he felt. "How do you 
know about that?" 
"I know you're planning to drive to Jefferson City tomorrow. And I know 
you won't be the only one interested in what your grandfather left behind." 
She reached into her bag and produced a plain white card. No logo. Just a 
phone number in black type, and below it, two words: WHEN READY. 
"Whatever's in that box — don't tell anyone you found it," Nadia said. "And 
don't go alone." 
"Why should I trust you?" 
"You shouldn't." Her smile again — not performed. Lived-in. "Trust no one. 
Question everything. Verify before you believe. It's the only thing that's kept your 
family alive this long." 
She walked away without looking back. Her footsteps made no sound on the 
floorboards that had announced Blake like a herald and let her pass like a ghost. 
Blake sat in the booth, holding the card, trying to understand why he felt seen. 
For the first time in years, someone had looked at him and recognized what they 
were looking at. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
Her paper trail was perfect. Too perfect. 
Blake spent four hours hunting through databases and archives, building a 
picture of Sister Nadia Volkov that should have reassured him and instead made 
his skin prickle with the awareness that everything he was finding had been 
designed to be found. 
Born 1994, Lviv, Ukraine. Orphaned at six. Raised by the Sisters of the Holy 
Mother, a convent outside Kyiv. Immigrated at sixteen. Full scholarship to 
Georgetown. PhD in Medieval History at twenty-five. 
Her dissertation: "Cognitive Transformation in Medieval Grimoires: The 
Notory Arts Reconsidered." The thesis argued that medieval grimoires were 

===== PAGE 25 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
25 
cognitive technologies. Systematic attempts to enhance human perception through 
geometric visualization. 
She'd researched exactly what Blake had experienced in the tunnels beneath 
SubTropolis. The patterns that had made his vision swim and his thoughts 
accelerate. 
Every detail confirmed. Every fact checkable. Perfect the way a legend is 
perfect. The way a cover identity is perfect when built by people who know what 
questions investigators will ask. 
But he couldn't stop thinking about her smile. The lived-in sadness of it. The 
calloused fingertips. The repaired bag stitching. Small details that didn't fit a 
manufactured identity. 
Either Sister Nadia Volkov was telling the truth, or she was very, very good 
at lying. 
Blake looked at the card. WHEN READY. 
Was he ready? 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
He left before dawn. The Missouri River Runner. Blake needed to be early, needed 
to walk the platform and watch who else was traveling east. 
The gray sedan was parked outside the station. Same car from yesterday, 
same tinted windows. They weren't trying to hide. 
The train followed the river. October morning. Harvested corn fields 
stretching to the horizon. Abandoned barns listing toward collapse. Small towns 
appearing and disappearing. Main Streets with more vacant storefronts than open 
businesses. 
Missouri in autumn. Beautiful and dying. 
He thought about Nadia's dissertation. About cognitive technologies and 
geometric enhancement. He thought about her hands. The way she'd said your 
grandfather with something closer to reverence than clinical interest. 
Don't go alone, she'd said. 
But Blake was alone. Had always been. Isolated by questions he couldn't stop 
asking. Sarah had left because it was exhausting. She'd been right. 
Except they weren't coincidences. 
The Missouri State Capitol dome appeared through the window. Jefferson 
City. A gray sedan idled in the station lot, different plates, same make, same tinted 
windows. 
They'd been waiting for him. 
Blake walked to First National Bank, carrying his grandfather's watch on his 
wrist and his father's questions in his head.

And Nadia's card in his pocket, warm from being held too long.

CHAPTER THREE 
THE BOX 
❧ 
Blake followed Patricia Thornton down a hallway marked PRIVATE. His hands 
were steady. The rest of him was not.31 
"Box 1847," Patricia said, unlocking a heavy door marked VAULT with a key 
and a code. "Established in 1968. Rental paid through December 2025 — fifty-
seven years in advance." She led him into a room lined with brass doors, the air 
cool and dry. "It would have lapsed in two months. But your grandfather was very 
specific in his instructions. Only one authorized heir." 
She pulled a card from her pocket. The handwriting was instantly 
recognizable. William's precise script, the same hand that had filled the margins of 
the Don Quixote Blake still kept on his nightstand. 
For Blake Masters. When he is ready. 
His hands started shaking. 
Patricia inserted two keys and turned them at once. The door swung open 
with a whisper of hinges that hadn't moved in decades.32 
A metal box, approximately twelve by eighteen by six inches deep. Patricia 
lifted it out, set it on the table. 
"I'll give you privacy," she said. "When you're finished, press the call button." 
She left. The vault door closed behind her with a sound like a period at the 
end of a sentence.33 
 
31 The chapter's title, 'The Accident,' operates on two levels: Blake's fall from the

32 The Moreau collection is fictional, but the novel's description of uncataloged

Seven notebooks. 
Black leather binding, aged to a deep brown almost approaching black. 
William's handwriting on the first page: 
Documentation. 1968-1998. WCM. 
Thirty years of documentation.35 
Blake lifted the first notebook carefully. The leather was soft, oiled over 
decades. He opened to a random page and saw geometric figures. Circles inscribed 
within circles, triangles nested at precise angles, lines extending into margins and 
wrapping around text in Latin and Greek and languages Blake didn't recognize. 
His eyes started to water. Not from emotion. From the patterns. They pulled 
at his vision the way certain resonances pull at the inner ear, made something deep 
in his neural architecture try to synchronize with something it wasn't designed for. 
The same sensation he'd felt in SubTropolis, amplified. Focused. Intentional. 
William hadn't just documented motifs. He'd been working with them. 
Blake closed the notebook. Breathed. 
Six more underneath. He didn't open them. Not here. His hands were 
shaking worse and his vision had gone soft at the edges, his brain straining toward 
a vibration it could almost reach.36

His father's facility. The launch control center where James Masters had

He walked through the residential streets east of the Capitol. Past the house

He thought about calling Nadia. 
Not for information. Not to report. He thought about calling because his 
hands were still shaking and the notebooks were heavy and somewhere in the past 
forty-eight hours the loneliness that had been background noise for six months 
had become a sound he couldn't ignore. He wanted to hear another human voice 
that didn't require him to explain himself. 
His thumb hovered over the call button. 
He put the phone away. 
Habit. The habit of carrying things alone, of being the person who noticed 
what others missed and learned not to mention it. The habit of isolation that had 
become so familiar it felt like personality. 
Blake found a bench near the Capitol building. The Missouri River was 
visible from here, brown and wide, October sunlight turning the current into 
moving proportion. He watched it without trying to read it. 
He opened the sealed envelope. 
Inside: a single sheet of paper, handwritten. William's script, smaller than 
usual, pressed closer together as if he'd been trying to fit a lifetime onto one page. 
Blake, 
If you're reading this, I'm gone. Your father is probably gone too — I've tried to protect 
him, but the patterns don't stop once you start seeing them. They pull you deeper. They consume 
you. They take everything. 
I saw them first from seventy thousand feet. U-2 reconnaissance, 1956. The mission was 
classified — photographing Soviet installations. But I saw something else. Patterns in cloud 
formations that matched mathematical sequences I'd seen in medieval manuscripts. Proportional 
relationships in view features that shouldn't exist. The higher I flew, the more I could see. And 
once I saw them, I couldn't unsee them. 
I spent thirty years documenting what I found. The notebooks will show you. The patterns 
exist at every scale — from quantum to cosmic. They're in cave paintings from 30,000 BC. 
They're in medieval grimoires. They're carved into bedrock beneath Kansas City. They're the 
operating system of presence, Blake. And some people — people like us — can perceive them. 

===== PAGE 31 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
31 
Your father has the gift. You have it too. I've watched you since you were nine years old, 
watching how you see things others miss. The cardinal on the branch. The model airplanes I hung 
in the garage. The patterns in flowstone at Meramec Caverns.40 
But the gift comes with a price. You know that already, don't you? Your brain sometimes 
can't keep up with what your eyes are telling it. The headaches. The sense that you're seeing more 
than you should. 
I think the symptoms are not disorder — they're adaptation. Your brain is trying to 
upgrade itself to process information it wasn't designed to handle. It hurts. It isolates you. It takes 
everything normal away. 
But maybe that's not a curse. Maybe it's the price of seeing. 
The seven notebooks document everything I learned. Read them carefully. Trust what you 
see, even when people tell you you're imagining things. Trust your brain, even when it betrays you. 
And when you're ready, when you've seen enough to understand, find the others. They've 
been waiting. 
I'm sorry I couldn't tell you this while I was alive. I'm sorry I left you alone with this. But 
you're not alone, Blake. You never were. 
Trust the patterns. Trust yourself. 
— Grandpa William 
P.S. The model airplanes in my garage. They're a map. You'll understand when you're 
ready. 
Blake read the letter three times. Folded it carefully. Put it back in the 
envelope. 
His hands had stopped shaking. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
The 2:15 Missouri River Runner pulled out of Jefferson City heading west. A gray 
sedan idled in the station lot. Different plates, same model, same tinted glass. 
Patient. Unhurried. 
The train followed the river. Blake watched the water and the fields, the same 
terrain he'd crossed that morning but different now. Everything looked different 
when you had a dead man's letter in your pocket telling you the world was more 
than it appeared. 
He reached for his phone again. Nadia's number on the screen. 
 
40 Meramec Caverns near Sullivan, Missouri is a real cave system that has been used

He should call. Tell her what he'd found. She'd know what the equipment tag

about the airplanes, the apology for leaving him alone. The sentence that had 
cracked something open: You're not alone. You never were. 
He wanted to read it to someone. To Nadia, specifically. 
Just call her, a voice that sounded like William's said. For God's sake, boy. Call her. 
He put the phone away. Watched Missouri scroll past the window. 
The higher I flew, the more I could see. 
Blake pressed his forehead against the glass. Cold. Outside, Missouri was 
beautiful as only dying things are. Leaves turning, fields emptied, the light going 
golden and horizontal. 
Three generations. William saw the sequences from seventy thousand feet 
and spent thirty years documenting them in secret. James found them 
underground and died asking questions. Blake had stumbled into them beneath 
SubTropolis and lost his career, his clearance, and the last person who'd tried to 
love him. 
He climbed the stairs to his apartment. Set the canvas bag on the kitchen 
table. The Breitling showed 6:47 PM. 
Seven notebooks. Thirty years. A dead man's permission to trust himself. 
And Nadia's number in his phone, still uncalled. The habit of carrying things 
alone already starting to feel less like personality and more like something he 
might, eventually, put down. 
Blake opened the first notebook. 
The patterns began to move.

CHAPTER FOUR 
THE ACCIDENT 
❧ 
Two days since Jefferson City.41 
The notebooks were in his apartment now. Seven of them, locked in the 
fireproof safe he'd bought at Home Depot the morning after. He hadn't read them 
yet — not really. He'd managed a few pages of the first before closing it, hands 
shaking, head pounding, something behind his eyes straining toward a vibration it 
couldn't quite reach. 
Whatever his grandfather had spent thirty years documenting, Blake's brain 
wasn't ready for it. 
But it couldn't leave them alone either. He dreamed about the mathematical 
figures. Circles and triangles rotating through dimensions that had no names. He'd 
wake at 3:47, as always, and sit at the kitchen table staring at the safe in the closet, 
feeling the notebooks like a low hum at the base of his skull. 
So here he was, back at Miller Nichols Library, pretending to work on his 
dissertation while his mind circled the contents of that safe. The notebooks had 
mentioned something. The Moreau collection. William had visited this library in 
1967, 1972, and 1985. Had made notes about manuscripts hidden in plain sight 
where Blake had spent five years of graduate school and never known to look. 
Blake closed his laptop. Walked toward the stacks.42 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
 
41 Chapter Five's opening — Nadia arriving with Thai food and bandages — is the 
novel's most effective domestication of extraordinary events. After the cosmic 
implications of Blake's perceptual awakening, the narrative pauses for takeout 
containers and antiseptic wipes. This tonal modulation prevents the novel from 
floating into abstraction. [Booth, The Rhetoric of Fiction (1961); Genette, Narrative Discourse 
(1983)] 
42 'The notae are keys that unlock those capabilities' — Nadia's framing of the Ars 
Notoria as cognitive technology rather than mysticism reflects genuine academic 
reassessment. Claire Fanger's 'Invoking Angels' (2012) and Julien Véronèse's work on 
the Ars Notoria argue for similar functional interpretations of medieval ritual texts. [Le 
Goff, Medieval Civilization (1988); Eco, The Name of the Rose (1980)] 

===== PAGE 34 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
34 
The Miller Nichols Library had secrets. 
Not official ones — no locked cages, no forbidden sections. But Blake had 
worked security long enough to recognize things hidden in plain sight. A shelf unit 
slightly out of alignment. Catalog entries that didn't match the physical collection. 
A door marked MECHANICAL that was too clean to house machinery. 
He started in Medieval History, where he'd spent most of his graduate career. 
But William's notebooks hadn't mentioned the canon. They'd mentioned 
something older. Something that shouldn't exist in a university library in Kansas 
City. 
Blake moved deeper. Medieval History gave way to Religious Studies. 
Religious Studies to Comparative Religion. And Comparative Religion gave way to 
something the catalog called "Special Collections (Uncataloged)" — a single shelf 
unit against a back wall where the fluorescent lights flickered and the air smelled 
like dust and forgetting. 
And at the top of the shelf, nearly hidden behind a row of bound journals 
from the 1890s, a book with no title at all.43 
Dark leather, worn smooth by centuries of hands. No text on the spine. No 
library markings. No call number. Nothing that indicated this book belonged to 
any official collection. 
Blake reached for the rolling ladder. Oak and brass, solid. He'd climbed it 
dozens of times. Tested the first rung. Climbed. 
Three rungs up, his fingers just brushing the dark leather binding, the rung 
beneath his foot gave way. 
Not cracked. Not broken. Just absent. As if the wood had decided to stop 
being solid. 
Blake grabbed for the shelf. Missed. His shoulder struck wood. His hip 
caught a corner. And then his head met the tile floor, and the world went white. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
Not black, white. 
Blake was aware of lying on his back. Cold tile beneath him. Pain blooming 
everywhere. But the pain was distant, his nervous system overwhelmed by input 
independent of the physical world. 
The white light was full of shapes. 
 
43 Picture Cave in Washington County, Missouri, is a real archaeological site 
containing pre-Columbian rock art. The cave's petroglyphs include geometric patterns 
that have been studied by Diaz-Granados and Duncan in their comprehensive survey 
of Missouri rock art. [Pastoureau, The Bear: History of a Fallen King (2007); Tattersall, Masters 
of the Planet (2012)] 

===== PAGE 35 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
35 
Proportional figures. Circles inscribed within circles, triangles nested at 
precise angles, rotating through dimensions his eyes couldn't track but his brain 
somehow followed. 
He knew these shapes. Had seen them in his grandfather's notebooks. In the 
carvings beneath SubTropolis. In the flowstone at Meramec Caverns that William 
had traced with his fingers seventeen years ago.44 
The shapes pulsed. Once. Twice. In rhythm with something deeper than 
heartbeat, some fundamental frequency beneath the ordinary world. 
See, something said. Not a voice. Information transmitted directly, bypassing 
his ears. 
See. 
The white began to fade. Cognition reassembled itself in fragments. The 
floor, the fluorescent lights, the smell of old books and his own blood where he'd 
bitten his tongue. 
Everything too sharp, too detailed, edges too crisp, as if someone had 
adjusted the resolution of reality. 
Something heavy pressed against his chest. 
The book. The one with no title. It had fallen with him. 
Blake opened the cover. No title page. Just pages covered in spatial 
arrangements. Circles within circles, triangles within triangles. The patterns 
moved. Or seemed to. Ink on paper. But for a moment he could have sworn he'd 
seen them rotate. 
He closed the book. Breathed. 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
The cave was cold and the flashlight beam caught dust motes that hung in the air 
like frozen stars. 
Blake was ten years old. His grandfather stood beside him in the darkness. 
Meramec Caverns. Tourist attraction outside Sullivan, Missouri. But William 
had found a side passage, always did, everywhere they went. 
"See the formations?" William's voice echoed off walls that had never felt 
artificial light. "See how they repeat?" 
 
44 The Kansas City metropolitan area sits atop one of the most extensive cave systems 
in North America. The Bethany Falls Limestone extends across Jackson, Clay, and 
Platte counties. Pre-Columbian petroglyphs have been documented in Missouri cave 
systems throughout the Ozarks and along the Missouri River valley. The novel's fiction 
of geometric carvings beneath SubTropolis draws on the real archaeological record of 
Missouri's extensive cave art traditions. [Missouri Archaeological Society records; Diaz-
Granados & Duncan, The Petroglyphs of Missouri (2000)]

Blake saw rock. Stalactites dripping from the ceiling. Stalagmites rising to 
meet them. "They're just shapes," he said. 
"Nothing in nature is random, Blake." William crouched, eyes level with his 
grandson's. He traced a finger along the stone curtain, following lines Blake 
couldn't see. 
"It's the same pattern as the water that carved it. The same as the streams on 
the surface. The same as the rivers." His eyes gleamed. "And that structure is the 
same as the veins in your body. The same as the branches of a tree. The same as 
lightning." 
"Goes all the way up?" 
"All the way down, too." William smiled. "Motifs repeat at every scale. 
Quantum to cosmic. Everything connects." 
He stood, turning the flashlight toward deeper darkness. 
"Most people never see it," he said quietly. "They walk through a world made 
of pattern and beauty, and they see chaos." He looked down at Blake. "But you'll 
see it. Someday." 
"How will I know when I'm ready?" 
"You'll see the patterns everywhere. And once you start, you won't be able to 
stop." 
"Then why would I want to see it?" 
"Because it's the truth." William's hand tightened on Blake's shoulder. "And 
once you know the truth, you can only choose whether to run from it or follow it 
wherever it leads." 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
Blake came back to himself on the library floor. The fluorescent lights buzzed at 
sixty hertz. He could hear the frequency now, separate it from the building's 
electrical system, from the HVAC, from the almost-inaudible pulse of data 
moving through network cables in the walls. 
He stood. The wallpaper was alive with structure. Hexagons tessellating. 
Fractals hiding in geometric repetition. The floor tiles. Below, the grain of the 
shelves. The dust motes in afternoon light. Everything connected. Everything 
singing. 
His hands were trembling. Not from the fall. From the input. He gripped the 
shelf beside him. The wood grain was a river system matching the bronchial 
pattern of his lungs, matching the root structure of trees outside, matching the 
drainage map of Missouri. The same geometry. Everywhere. 
A librarian at the end of the aisle. "Sir? Are you all right?" 
"I'm fine. The ladder. I fell." 

===== PAGE 37 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
37 
"You're bleeding." 
Blake touched his head. Hand came away red. "Just need to clean up." 
He looked at the ladder. The rung was intact. Solid oak. No cracks. It should 
not have broken. 
But it had. And something had broken open. 
He tucked the book under his arm and walked toward the exit. The corridor 
was different. Below, the same corridor he'd walked a thousand times, but now 
the architectural proportions revealed themselves. The ratio was 1.618. The golden 
mean. His brain was reading it the way a musician reads sheet music. 
October air hit him sharp and clean. Everything was different. The clouds 
formed fractals. The trees repeated themselves in the Fibonacci sequence. A 
student crossed the quad ahead of him, headphones on, her walking gait a 
pendulum oscillation. The crowd moving in patterns that looked random from the 
surface but from Blake's new elevation revealed themselves as coordinated. 
He couldn't turn it off. 
Across the street, the gray sedan. 
He tried Andrew first. 
Andrew picked up on one ring. "Blake. What's up." 
"Something happened." Blake heard his own voice. Too fast, too bright. "In 
the library. I fell, and now I can. The patterns, Andrew. They're everywhere. In the 
trees, the clouds, the spacing of the streetlights. Everything is connected and I can 
see the connections." 
A pause. The typing stopped. 
"Describe what you're seeing," Andrew said. Not alarmed. Analytical. "Be 
specific." 
"Spatial relationships. The ratio between the lamp posts. It's the same as the 
branching angle on that oak tree, which is the same as the Fibonacci sequence. 
The same form at every scale." 
"Fractal self-similarity. Okay, that's consistent with heightened pattern 
recognition. I can build a model of that. Map the ratios, run them against known 
fractal dimensions, see if your perception matches mathematical reality or if it's 
pareidolia." 
Blake closed his eyes. Opened them. The patterns were still there. 
"It's not pareidolia," he said. 
"You hit your head. Let me quantify this before you decide it's real." 
Andrew was right. Methodologically. But the rightness was beside the point. 
Like describing a symphony by listing the frequencies. 
"Build the model. I'll send you the ratios." 
"Good. And go to urgent care. You probably have a concussion." 

===== PAGE 38 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
38 
He hung up. Andrew would build something useful. He always did. But what 
Blake needed was someone who'd say I know what you're seeing and mean it from the 
inside. 
Blake reached for his phone. 
Two days he'd been carrying Nadia's number like a talisman. 
The habit broke. 
He pressed call. 
She picked up immediately. Not "hello" — just silence, as if she'd been 
expecting this. 
"It's Blake." 
"I know." A pause. "Are you all right?" 
"No. Something happened. In the library. I fell, and —" He stopped. How 
did you explain that reality had changed resolution? "I need to show you 
something. My grandfather's notebooks. And a book I found. I can't process this 
alone." 
"Where are you?" 
"UMKC. Outside the library." 
"Go home. Lock the door. Don't open the notebooks until I'm there." Her 
voice had shifted, urgency underneath the composure. "I'll come to you." 
"Nadia —" 
"Don't read the notebooks alone, Blake." She said his name differently than 
she'd said Mr. Masters. Softer. Her accent warming the vowel. "The patterns in 
those pages. They're not passive. They're designed to do something to the person 
looking at them. You need someone with you." 
"You could have told me that three days ago." 
"You wouldn't have believed me three days ago." Another pause. "You've 
seen them now, haven't you. The motifs. In everything." 
Blake looked at the quad. Fractal clouds. Fibonacci trees. "Yes." 
"Then you're ready." He heard her stand. The sound of a bag being 
shouldered. "I'll be there in an hour. Blake —" She stopped. Started again, and 
what she said wasn't what she'd planned to say. "It's going to be all right." 
It was such a human thing to say. So far from the surveillance craft and the 
manufactured paper trail. 
"I'll be here," Blake said. 
He hung up. Walked home. Twenty-two blocks through a world singing in a 
language he was only beginning to understand, his head bleeding and the untitled 
book pressed against his ribs and Nadia's voice still in his ear, saying his name the 
way she'd said it. 
Softer. Warmer. The accent on the vowel. 
Like she'd been waiting to say it for a long time.

CHAPTER FIVE 
THE REVELATION 
❧ 
Nadia knocked at 7:15 PM.45 
Blake had spent the hour trying to clean up — not the apartment, which was 
beyond help, but himself. Washed the blood from his hair. Changed his shirt. 
Pressed a cold washcloth against the cut until the swelling went down enough that 
he didn't look like he'd been in a bar fight. 
He still looked like he'd been in a bar fight.46 
When he opened the door, Nadia stood in the hallway holding two paper 
bags. One from a Thai restaurant Blake recognized. The other from a pharmacy. 
"You haven't eaten," she said. Not a question. 
"How did you —" 
"Because you never eat when you're processing something." She walked past 
him, set the bags on the kitchen table. "Pad Thai. Tom Kha. And bandages, 
because whatever you did to your forehead needs more than a washcloth." 
She turned and looked at him properly. In the apartment light, without the 
coffee shop's theatrical shadows, she was. Blake searched for the word. present. 
More present than anyone had been in his space since Sarah had packed her things 
and left a silence behind that Blake had learned to treat as furniture. 
"Sit down," she said. "I need to look at that cut." 
"You're a nun, not a nurse." 
"I'm a woman who grew up in a Ukrainian orphanage." She pulled antiseptic 
and butterfly bandages from the pharmacy bag. "Sit." 
 
45 The West Bottoms is a historic industrial district in Kansas City, adjacent to the 
Kansas-Missouri state line. Formerly the city's meatpacking and warehouse center, it 
contains limestone-clad buildings dating to the 1880s. The Foundation's fictional 
location at 2847 Cherry Street places it in the district's most atmospheric zone. [Brown 
& Dorsett, K.C.: A History of Kansas City (1978)] 
46 Cherry Street establishes the Foundation's physical character: a repurposed cold 
storage facility built on limestone bedrock. The building's industrial past (storing 
things) becomes its metaphorical present (storing knowledge). The limestone 
foundation is both literal and thematic. [Anheier & Toepler, International Encyclopedia of 
Civil Society (2010)] 

===== PAGE 41 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
41 
 
Figure 2. Ars Notoria page — the 'notae' (geometric meditation figures) that the novel 
reinterprets as cognitive technology rather than mystical invocation. 
"It's not deep," she said. "But you hit hard. How's your vision?" 
"Different." 
Her hands paused. "Different how?" 
"Everything has structure. Geometry. Relationships I couldn't see before. 
The patterns from the notebooks, from SubTropolis, they're everywhere now." 
Nadia didn't look skeptical. She looked relieved. 
"Good," she said quietly, and pressed a butterfly bandage across the cut. 
"That means it's working." 
"What's working?" 
She finished the bandage. Stepped back. And for a moment, standing in his 
kitchen with antiseptic on her fingers and Thai food getting cold on the table, she 
looked less like an operative and more like a person who'd been carrying 
something heavy for a long time and had just set part of it down. 
"We need to eat first," she said. "Then I need to show you something." 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 


===== PAGE 42 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
42 
They ate cross-legged on the kitchen floor because the table was too small and the 
couch was covered in manuscripts. Nadia used chopsticks with unselfconscious 
ease. Blake used a fork and tried not to stare. 
A bottle of wine stood on the counter. Sarah's Grüner Veltliner, Austrian, 
unopened for six months. It caught the kitchen light and threw a pale green 
shadow across the wall. Blake noticed himself noticing and tried to stop. Failed. 
This was his life now. Every surface a text. Every shadow a calculation. 
Nadia's presence changed the apartment's resonance. She occupied space the 
way music occupied a concert hall, altering everything simply by being there. 
"So," Blake said. "You've been watching me since I was fifteen." 
"Not me personally. The organization." Nadia picked up a piece of tofu. "I've 
been watching you for about two years. Since your SubTropolis discovery." 
"Two years. And you waited until now?" 
"You weren't ready until now." She met his eyes. "When the bank called you, 
when you started moving, that's when we knew the timeline had accelerated." 
"We." 
"The Keepers." She said it simply, the way you'd say the department. "We've 
been operating in Kansas City since 1924. Protecting this knowledge. Guiding 
those who are ready to receive it." 
"Since Father Moreau." 
Her composure cracked. "Since Father Moreau was murdered. Yes." 
Blake thought about his father. About James Masters, dead on a straight road 
in perfect weather. Nadia saw the thought cross his face. 
"The Keepers don't kill," she said. "There are others who do. The Custodians 
believe humanity isn't ready. The Completion Sect believes the knowledge should 
be weaponized. And then there's us. We believe people should be allowed to 
choose." 
"Choose what?" 
"Whether to see. Whether to pursue the transformation or stay as they are." 
She set down her chopsticks. "Free will, Blake. Informed consent. That's why your 
grandfather documented everything." 
"Show me what you need to show me," he said. 
Nadia pulled the untitled book from Blake's bag. She handled it the way a 
priest handles a relic. Opened it on the kitchen floor between them, their knees 
almost touching. 
"Do you know what the Ars Notoria is?" she asked. 
"A medieval grimoire. Memory enhancement, eloquence. Most scholars 
consider it superstitious nonsense." 
"Most scholars are wrong." Nadia traced her finger along one of the 
geometric figures. "The Ars Notoria isn't magic. It's technology. Cognitive 

===== PAGE 43 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
43 
technology. These figures. The notae. Are interfaces. Tools for accessing parts of 
presence that are normally dormant." 
She looked up. Their faces were close. 
"Your brain is capable of far more than you normally access," she said. "The 
notae are keys that unlock those capabilities. When your brain processes these 
specific mathematical relationships, it fires in sequences it doesn't normally use. 
Those sequences activate dormant neural pathways." 
"Enhanced perception. Pattern recognition." 
"Yes. But here's what matters." Nadia turned pages carefully, stopping at a 
figure more complex than the others. "The conventional history says this 
technology was developed by medieval monks building on Greek and Arabic 
sources. Your grandfather found evidence that contradicts that." 
She showed him photographs on her phone. Cave art. Circles within circles. 
Nested triangles. The same proportions as the figures in the book. 
"Picture Cave," she said. "Washington County, Missouri. Dated to at least a 
thousand years ago. Pre-Columbian. Pre-European. Pre-Christian." 
"The patterns were here first," Blake said slowly. "Everything in medieval 
Europe was copied from North American sources." 
"That's what your grandfather believed. What thirty years of documentation 
supports." Nadia's eyes were bright. This wasn't the operative or the nun. This was 
the scholar. "If we can prove it, we could rewrite the history of human awareness." 
"And the Custodians want to stop that." 
"The Custodians killed your father to stop that." 
The words hung between them. Nadia held his gaze. Her hand on the book 
had gone still, her ring finger pressing against the silver band. 
"I'm sorry," she said. "I know that's —" 
"Don't. Don't apologize for telling me what I already know." 
Silence. Two people sitting on a kitchen floor with a medieval manuscript 
between them and a dead man's questions filling the space. 
Then Nadia laughed. 
Not at him. At the situation. At the two of them cross-legged with pad Thai 
containers and antiseptic wipes and a book that shouldn't exist. 
It was the first time Blake had heard her laugh. It changed her entire face, 
broke the composure, revealed something younger underneath. 
"What?" Blake said. 
"Nothing. It's just. I've been preparing for this conversation for two years. I 
had it planned. Professional. Controlled." She shook her head. "Not on a kitchen 
floor." 
"You wanted a PowerPoint presentation." 
"I wanted to seem competent." 

===== PAGE 44 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
44 
"You showed up with Thai food and bandages. That's competent enough." 
She smiled. Not the sad, knowing smile from the coffee shop. Something 
warmer. Something that reached her eyes. 
"There's more to show you," she said. "A place. Our operational base. But it 
can wait until tomorrow." 
"Why tomorrow?" 
"Because you have a concussion. Because you haven't slept. Because —" She 
hesitated. "Because I'd rather stay here tonight. If that's. I can sleep on the couch. 
I just don't think you should be alone right now." 
It was a reasonable thing to say. Clinical, even. But Nadia's hand was still on 
the book between them, and her fingers were two inches from Blake's knee, and 
the way she'd said I'd rather stay carried a weight apart from concussion protocol. 
"The couch is covered in manuscripts," Blake said. 
"I'll move them." 
"Blake." She looked at him steadily. "I'm offering to stay because you 
shouldn't be alone. That's all." 
"I know." 
"Good." She stood. Began clearing the Thai containers. "Show me which 
manuscripts I'm allowed to move." 
✦  ⊕  ✦ 
Blake didn't sleep. Not really. He lay in the dark, the ceiling patterns moving above 
him, and listened to Nadia breathing in the next room. 
She'd cleared a space on the couch with the efficiency of someone used to 
making order from chaos. Had arranged manuscripts into careful stacks, handling 
them with the same reverence she'd given the untitled book. 
Now she was asleep, or pretending to be, and Blake lay in the dark and 
thought about what she'd told him. Keepers. Custodians. Completion Sect. Three 
organizations fighting over ancient knowledge. His grandfather had found the 
evidence. His father had died pursuing it. 
But that wasn't what kept him awake. 
What kept him awake was the laugh. The startled sound of Nadia laughing 
on his kitchen floor. The way she'd said I wanted to seem competent with a self-
awareness that cut through every layer of craft and left something honest standing 
in the kitchen light. 
At 3:47, Blake's eyes opened. He lay still, listening. Nadia's breathing from 
the couch. The refrigerator hum. The motifs on the ceiling, visible even without 
light. 
He got up. Went to the kitchen. 

===== PAGE 45 =====
BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency 
45 
She was already awake. 
"Can't sleep either?" Soft. Not startled. She'd heard him get up, both of them 
tuned to frequencies most people couldn't hear. 
"3:47," Blake said. "Every morning." 
"William mentioned that in Notebook 3. A consistent wake time associated 
with the integration process." A pause. "Two cups?" 
Blake made two cups. Brought one to the couch. Nadia sat up, the blanket 
around her shoulders, her hair loose for the first time. She'd worn it pulled back 
every time he'd seen her. Loose, it fell past her shoulders, dark in the pre-dawn 
kitchen. 
She took the cup. Their fingers brushed on the ceramic. 
"Tomorrow," Nadia said. "I'll take you to Cherry Street. The operational 
base. There's more to show you. Maps, connections, your grandfather's full 
research. And we need to talk about what comes next." 
"What comes next?" 
She turned the cup in her hands. The silver ring caught the early light. 
"SubTropolis," she said. "Eventually. When you're ready. But first, you need 
to learn to control what you're seeing. Your grandfather documented his entire 
process, exercises, practices. It took him months." 
"Months." 
"Months." She looked at him. In the gray light, her green eyes were dark, 
almost black. "You're not alone in this, Blake. I know the sequences make 
everything seem distant. Like you're seeing the world from behind glass. But 
you're not alone." 
She said it the way she'd said it's going to be all right on the phone. As if it were 
fact and not comfort. 
"Thank you," he said. 
"For what?" 
"For the Thai food." 
She laughed again. Quiet this time, almost private. 
"You're welcome," she said. 
And freshly, in months — maybe years. The 3:47 AM silence in Blake's 
apartment felt like something other than loneliness. 
✦  ⊕  ✦

BOOK ONE  —  The Inheritance of Frequency
